> **🧰 SESSION-31 ENVIRONMENT NOTES (each of these cost real time):**
> - **🔴 DO NOT part-clean `target/` before a multi-crate gate run. Wipe it.** The founder said so; I pushed back to save rebuild time and was wrong. Keeping ~90 GB of `target/debug` and then running `cargo clean -p` on 5 crates made cargo rebuild them **on top of** what was there: `target/` grew to **146.8 GB**, free space hit **1.1 GB**, and four gates died on `os error 112 (not enough space)` — with **zero test failures anywhere in the log**. The whole run had to be redone. `cargo clean -p` frees almost nothing and costs a rebuild; it is not a disk strategy. **Escalation order when a full gate run is coming: wipe `target/` entirely.** A cold `build --workspace` is **69.3 min** measured — a known, bounded cost, and far cheaper than a 6-hour run that dies at the last gate.
> - **📉 Measured disk burn per gate, cold:** `build --workspace` produced the base tree, then each test suite added ~20-35 GB of test binaries — 159.4 → 139.5 → 104.8 → 74.5 → 43.1 → 35 GB, with `clippy --all-targets` costing only ~6.6 GB because it reuses build artifacts. **Budget ~130 GB free for a full cold gate run**; the whole thing finished at 28.4 GB.
> - **🐳 `docker system prune` DOES NOT GIVE THE DISK BACK — you must compact the vhdx.** Founder called this before we hit it. After a full prune, `docker system df` read **0 B** for images, containers, volumes AND build cache while `C:\Users\shahb\AppData\Local\Docker\wsl\disk\docker_data.vhdx` still occupied **45.86 GB**. The space is freed *inside* the virtual disk and never returned to Windows. **Fix:** stop Docker Desktop → `wsl --shutdown` → elevated `diskpart` `select vdisk` / `attach vdisk readonly` / `compact vdisk` / `detach vdisk`. Took **6 seconds** and returned **42.65 GB** (45.86 → 3.21 GB). `Optimize-VHD` is NOT available on Windows 11 Home, so diskpart is the route. Script preserved at `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\compact-docker-vhdx.ps1`.
> - **⏱️ Full cold DoD gate run = 5h51m** (build 69.3 + storage 41.1 + consolidator 60.9 + retrieval 52.3 + app 63.1 + tauri 28.9 + clippy 35.1 + fmt ~0). Budget accordingly; run it detached and overnight.
> - **A cold build NEEDS vcvars64 sourced** or native deps (openssl-src, llama.cpp, DuckDB) fail. `C:\Program Files (x86)\Microsoft Visual Studio\2019\BuildTools\VC\Auxiliary\Build\vcvars64.bat`; the gate script imports it via `cmd /c "<bat> && set"` and verifies `cl.exe` lands on PATH before starting.
> - **⚠️ `*>>` redirection writes cargo output as UTF-16BE while `Out-File -Encoding utf8` writes UTF-8 — the gate log ends up MIXED-ENCODING** and every naive `Select-String` silently returns zero hits. Read it with `[System.Text.Encoding]::BigEndianUnicode.GetString(bytes)` or the log will look empty and clean when it is neither. **Do not conclude "no warnings" from a grep that found nothing until the encoding is confirmed.**
> - **📌 The gate script that produced this run is worth reusing:** `scratchpad/gates2.ps1` — strictly serial, `-j 2`, vcvars sourced, per-step disk+RAM logging, a **12 GB low-disk abort** so exhaustion fails by name instead of as four mystery linker errors, and it **keeps going after a failure** so one broken gate cannot hide the other seven.
>
> **🧰 SESSION-30 ENVIRONMENT NOTES:**
> - **`target/debug/incremental` is the safe thing to delete when disk is tight — NOT `target/debug`.** Disk opened this session at **22.9 GB free** with `target/debug` at 93.5 GB (`deps` 55.4 · `build` 26.1 · `incremental` 7.3). Deleting `incremental` alone recovered **5.5 GB (→ 28.4 GB free)** and invalidated **nothing**: cargo checks fingerprints, so a crate that is already fresh is not rebuilt just because its incremental scratch is gone; the cache only makes crates that *do* rebuild slower. Deleting `target/debug` wholesale, by contrast, converts a ~15-minute incremental build into a cold multi-hour one — the wrong trade for a small change. **Escalation order when disk is tight: `incremental` → `target/release` (11.7 GB; safe *only because* the shippable MSI is preserved at `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\`, but a release rebuild costs hours + the vcvars dance) → `deps` last.**
> - **`cargo check --workspace --all-targets -j 2` = 1m36s** and caught nothing this time, which is the point — it is cheap insurance before committing the long gates.
> - **⚡ `candle.exe` is already on this box — use it.** `C:\Users\shahb\AppData\Local\tauri\WixTools314\candle.exe` compiles a `.wxs` in **seconds**, so every installer-fragment edit can be syntax- and ICE-checked without paying for a bundle. It also emits the `.wixobj` as readable XML, so you can confirm what a `<CustomAction>` actually compiled to (type code, sequence) before building anything.
> - **📖 Read a built MSI's tables without installing it** via the `WindowsInstaller.Installer` COM object (`OpenDatabase` read-only → `OpenView`/`Execute`/`Fetch`). This is how ADR-SEC-006 confirmed the custom action, its condition and its sequence number survived into the shipped MSI. Cheap, non-destructive, and the only way to catch a silently-dropped WiX fragment.
> - **🔐 ~~The agent session is NOT elevated, and `Start-Process -Verb RunAs` is unreliable here.~~ ⚠️ HALF WRONG — CORRECTED SESSION 31.** The agent session is indeed **not** elevated (`IsInRole(Administrator)` = False), and the `!` prefix does not help — it runs in this same unelevated session. **But `Start-Process -Verb RunAs` WORKS**: session 31 used it to run the ADR-SEC-006 verification first try, elevated, with the founder accepting the on-screen UAC prompt. Session 30's "no run at all" had nothing to do with UAC — **the target script was unparseable** (BOM-less UTF-8 + em-dashes → CP1252 curly quotes → tokenizer sees a string terminator; see ADR-SEC-006). The surviving half of the lesson is the important half: **do not assume a launched elevated process ran — check for its output before believing it.** The reliable way to tell *never started* from *started and died* is a wrapper that writes a marker on its first line: `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\run-elevated-wrapper.ps1`. Elevation needs the founder present to accept the prompt, so launch it while he is in the loop.
> - **✍️ ANY PowerShell script written for a LATER session must be PURE ASCII.** Our prose style puts em-dashes and curly quotes everywhere, and the Write tool emits BOM-less UTF-8; PowerShell 5.1 then reads the file as CP1252, where `E2 80 94` ends in `0x94` = `”`, **which the tokenizer treats as a string delimiter**. Result: the script dies at parse time having executed nothing, and the failure looks exactly like "it never launched". This cost two sessions. **Check before preserving any `.ps1`:** `([System.IO.File]::ReadAllBytes($f) | Where-Object { $_ -gt 127 }).Count` must be `0`, and `[System.Management.Automation.PSParser]::Tokenize((Get-Content $f -Raw), [ref]$e)` must yield `$e.Count -eq 0`. Same applies to any `.ps1` written into the scratchpad for a detached gate run.
> - **⏱️ Release build cost, measured (incremental, NOT cold):** `cargo build --release -p vault-cli -j 2` **36m37s** + `cargo tauri build` **34m42s** = **~71 minutes** to a 204.7 MB MSI. Nothing native rebuilt (llama.cpp/openssl/lance stayed cached) because only Rust crates had changed — vcvars was sourced anyway as cheap insurance per ADR-091. Budget ~1h10m for any MSI respin.
> - **Gate runs are launched DETACHED via `Start-Process powershell -File <script>.ps1`**, with the script written to the scratchpad, using `*>` redirection rather than `2>&1`. This dodges two known traps at once: the tool's 10-minute background cap killing a long build, and PowerShell 5.1 wrapping native-command stderr into `NativeCommandError` noise ([[feedback_cargo_on_windows_use_powershell]]). Keep the scratchpad path space-free so `-ArgumentList` quoting stays trivial.
>
> **🧰 SESSION-29 ENVIRONMENT NOTES (each of these cost real time this session):**
> - **`cargo test -p vault-consolidator` at the default `jobs = 4` OOMs this box.** It links **12 separate test binaries**, each pulling the full dep graph; four concurrent `link.exe` peaked at ~2 GB each (~6.9 GB together), driving free RAM to **0.31 GB** and committed to **45.3 / 54.4 GB**. Symptom is not a crash — it is the compile *crawling* while Windows swaps. **Use `cargo test -p vault-consolidator -j 2`.** Same for `clippy --all-targets`. `.cargo/config.toml`'s `jobs = 4` was calibrated on the BUILD phase, which links far fewer binaries; the test phase needs half that. Recovery that worked: kill `cargo`/`rustc`/`link`, `wsl --shutdown`, re-run — cargo resumes from cached artifacts rather than restarting.
> - **⚠️ Read free-RAM twice before concluding anything.** A single sample during a link peak read 0.49 GB; seconds later, linkers done, it read 6.46 GB. One reading is a snapshot of a spike, not the trend.
> - **`cargo build -p <crate>` does NOT reuse the `--workspace` cache.** Building a subset resolves features differently, so `cargo build -p vault-cli` rebuilt lancedb/lance and took **25m12s** where `--workspace` took 12m01s. For a quick binary rebuild, still use `cargo build --workspace`.
> - **`cargo check --workspace --all-targets` first is worth it** — 53.8s, and it catches every compile error before committing ~80 minutes to the real gates.
> - **`Start-Process -ArgumentList` does NOT quote array elements.** Every path through `C:\Projects\GitHub\Memory Vault` has a space and gets split into a bogus subcommand. Pre-quote each argument and `-join " "`.
>
> **🧰 SESSION-28 ENVIRONMENT NOTES (save the next session real time):**
> - **`target/debug` was deleted this session** to reclaim disk (122 GB → free space went 35.5 → 140 GB). It has since been rebuilt. `target/release` was KEPT deliberately — it holds the shippable MSI (`bundle/msi/Memory Vault_0.2.0_x64_en-US.msi`), which per ADR-091 can only be built locally.
> - **Preserved outside the repo at `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\`:** the MSI, and `vault-cli-b223f34-debug.exe` — the pre-ADR-096 binary that **reproduces the bug**, useful for before/after demonstrations without a rebuild.
> - **Cold `cargo test -p vault-consolidator` took ~35 min; `build --workspace` 25m24s.** Run every cargo invocation DETACHED via `Start-Process` — the tool's 10-minute background cap will otherwise kill a long build mid-way (it did, once, at the very last crate).
> - **RAM is the binding constraint, not disk.** A build was started with **0.9 GB free** and would very likely have died at link; closing an emulator/VM + a Java app + WSL freed ~2.8 GB. **Check free RAM before any long build**; target ≥3–4 GB.
> - `S:\` on this box is a `subst` alias to `C:\Projects\GitHub\Sandoq Kin` (a different project). Not ours, harmless, consumes no disk.

> **⚠️ A METHOD NOTE WORTH KEEPING (session 27).** The "Save schedule" bug was found because the app log said **nothing at all**. `set_maintenance_schedule` logs only on failure, so silence + an absent OS task was the whole signal. Two lessons: (a) when a command reports success and the world didn't change, check what the command was actually *told* (here: `enabled=false` → `unregister` → correctly succeeded) before suspecting the backend; (b) `build_spec` fails with `?` and **no log line at all** — a genuine silent-failure path that survived this session only because it wasn't hit. Worth a tracing line next time that file is open.

> ### 🕒 PRE-BETA FEATURE (founder directive, 2026-07-23) — user-configurable scheduled task ("cron job")
> **What the founder asked for:** before launching beta, add a scheduled-task feature the USER sets up. It gets **its own tab** in the app AND **a page during onboarding**, and must work on **Windows, macOS, and Linux**. The user opts in and picks the schedule — it is NOT auto-on.
> **What it runs (CONFIRM at design time — this is the key open question):** the natural periodic job is the nightly **sleep consolidator** (BRD §5.6) — running `vault-cli consolidate run` on a schedule so the vault self-maintains. ⚠️ **Dependency:** consolidation is currently NOT wired on the desktop (`phi4_model_path is None; consolidator not wired`). So decide first: (a) this feature schedules consolidation → we must wire Phi-4 first (adds scope + a 2.3 GB first-run download); or (b) for beta it schedules a lighter periodic task and consolidation-scheduling lands later. **Do not build the scheduler until this is answered.**
> **Cross-platform mechanism (no "cron exists" assumption — it doesn't on Windows):** Windows = Task Scheduler (`schtasks`/COM), macOS = `launchd` LaunchAgent plist, Linux = `cron` or a systemd user timer. The app generates + registers the correct one per-OS; the thing it schedules is the shipped `vault-cli` (already on PATH via the installer, ADR-091). Model as a `vault-core` trait (e.g. `Scheduler`) with three per-OS impls, per BRD §2 (communicate via traits, no cycles).
> **Security / UX open questions (resolve at design, write security tests first per §11):** (1) prefer per-USER scheduled tasks so registration needs **no elevation** (a UAC prompt during onboarding is a bad first impression); (2) the scheduled run MUST respect the single-writer constraint — it cannot collide with an open app or a connected MCP agent ([[cross-agent-mcp-connection]]); design a lock/skip-if-busy; (3) every scheduled run writes an audit row (BRD §11.9.1); (4) **white-label the UI** (ADR-086) — call it "automatic maintenance"/"scheduled consolidation", never "cron"/"Task Scheduler"; (5) the onboarding page lets the user pick a time + opt in/out, and must be skippable (not everyone wants it); (6) uninstall must remove the registered task (installer/uninstaller concern — see ADR-091 fragment).
> **Status (session 26): ✅ BUILT END-TO-END — see ADR-092 / ADR-093 / ADR-SEC-005 above.** The "what it runs" question was answered by the founder: **go big — real consolidation via Phi-4** (`vault-cli consolidate run`), NOT a lighter task. The design questions below are resolved: per-user tasks / no elevation ✅; single-writer respected via the vault-owner lock (busy → skip) ✅; audit row per state-changing command ✅; white-label UI ✅; onboarding page opt-in + skippable ✅. **OPEN:** uninstall-removes-the-task (ADR-SEC-005), and founder live-verify of the UI. The design detail below is retained as historical context of the directive.
>

> ### ⚠️ READ THIS BEFORE TRUSTING ANY "NON-OBVIOUS" WARNING IN AN OPENER
>
> The session-22 opener's item 3 warned that `AppConfig` is built once so a late download *"will NOT take effect until relaunch"*, and called it *"the single most likely thing to get silently wrong"*. **That was wrong, and acting on it would have produced real but unnecessary work** (re-init path / reordered `Application` construction). Five minutes of reading `reranker_lazy.rs` falsified it: construction does zero disk I/O and the model loads on first use. **The lesson is not "openers are unreliable" — it is that a warning about runtime behaviour is a hypothesis until the call graph is read.** The correction, the evidence, and what the real hazard turned out to be are in ADR-089 below.
>
> ### ✅ DONE THIS SESSION (don't rebuild it)
>
> - **`ensure_recall_engine`** (`commands/engine.rs`, NEW) — no-parameter Tauri command, `OnceCell`-deduped, retry-safe, opaque error code. Started on EVERY launch (returning users have no gate), awaited by the onboarding gate.
> - **Progress** — `ensure_model_at_path_with_progress` (vault-llm; plain form is now a wrapper, **Phi-4's caller untouched**) → `ensure_reranker_with_progress` (aggregate across both files) → `recall-engine://progress` Tauri event → fourth boot-check row in `dist/app.js`.
> - **ADR-089 degrade** — absent model → `VaultError::ModelUnavailable` → both rerank consumers fall back to retriever order. Tampered model → unchanged, still fatal.
> - **Unconditional reranker binding** in `main.rs` — this is what makes a mid-session download take effect with no relaunch.
> - **Guards** — `frontend_contract.rs` now covers the new command's four-file wiring plus the progress event name across the language boundary (a rename would leave the download working and the bar silently frozen).
>
> ### ✅ LIVE-VERIFIED 2026-07-22 (session 24) — session 23's work has now been RUN
>
> Measured on the founder's machine, returning-user path (`mv_onboarded=true`), reranker absent from `<data>/models/`:
>
> | Claim | Result |
> |---|---|
> | Download triggers with **no onboarding gate** (returning user) | ✅ `recall engine acquisition starting` 3s after boot |
> | Both files fetched + SHA-256 verified | ✅ 1.19 GB in **108s** (inside the 96-131s band) |
> | **Search works degraded mid-download** | ✅ `WARN … degraded, not failed; ADR-089`, results returned |
> | **Ranker engages with NO relaunch** | ✅ `Qwen3RerankerProvider opened` 15:07:18, bound absent at 14:48:51 |
> | Correct answers once loaded | ✅ founder ran "work" + "keyboard" |
> | Warm launch performs no re-download | ✅ zero download lines; **16.6s** integrity verify only |
>
> **⚠️ TWO CORRECTIONS TO THE SESSION-23 VERIFICATION LIST — it would have produced a false pass.**
> 1. **Do NOT verify this via `scripts/run-desktop-dev.ps1` as written.** It sets `VAULT_RERANK_MODEL_PATH`/`_TOKENIZER_PATH` to the **test fixtures**, and `main.rs::resolve_reranker_paths` prefers that env override. The app would download 1.19 GB into `<data>/models/` and then rank from the fixtures anyway — progress would look right and ADR-089's central claim would be **completely untested**. Use `-NoReranker`, which post-ADR-089 no longer disables anything: it resolves to the PRODUCTION download path. **That script's `-NoReranker` docs are now stale** (tech-debt below).
> 2. **"Warm launch shows NO progress row" was never true.** `ensure_reranker_with_progress` fires `on_progress` unconditionally after each file **even on a cache hit** (`model_fetch.rs`, the two trailing calls), so the row IS created on a warm run. It is invisible only because `#checks` lives inside the hidden `#screen-welcome` (`index.html:19`). It becomes visible on the **replay-welcome** path. Superseded by ADR-090, which reports real readiness instead of suppressing the row.

Skipped on a cost/benefit call: the expensive half of that risk (absent model breaking search) is now PROVEN fixed, and the remainder is a cosmetic error row costing 1.15 GB of re-download to exercise. Also re-run `cargo test -p vault-tauri --test reranker_download -- --ignored --nocapture` after any URL/hash change.

>
> **Still true from session 22 (don't rebuild):** `model_fetch::ensure_reranker` (cold 96.5-130.9s, warm 20.3s, real 1.15 GB) · `scripts/run-desktop-dev.ps1` (`-NoReranker` to A/B) · `tests/reranker_download.rs`.

> **📐 FOUNDER DECISION (2026-07-20) — download OVERLAPS onboarding.** First call was a plain blocking wait; he then refined it and the refinement is the design: the user connects their agent, creates boundaries, adds a first memory — work they must do anyway — while the fetch completes behind it. Same guarantee (nothing runs on a degraded engine once they actually use the vault), no dead time. Founder: *"the first time user is still going to take time setting up stuff like connecting with agent etc... which can also buy us time while the backend re-ranker or heavy stuff loads."* **IMPLEMENTED this session**, with one deliberate softening: on download FAILURE the gate lets the user through degraded rather than trapping them (ADR-087 requirement (b)) — safe only because of ADR-089's degrade. Fallback if slow links make the wait visible anyway: say so honestly on the opening screen, and note Managed/BYOK tiers have no local model to fetch at all — a real tier difference, not a manufactured one.

> ### 📦 THEN — MSI packaging (unchanged blockers, plus what session 22 learned)
>
> - **(a) `tauri.conf.json` `bundle` block is STALE — verified stale, not assumed.** `shortDescription`/`longDescription` still say **"V0.1 internal alpha"** and cite **ADR-010 (LanceDB plaintext-on-disk)**, which was FIXED in `d556b97` — so the installer would tell a beta user their vector store is plaintext when it is encrypted. Wrong in the trust-destroying direction for this product. `version` is `0.1.0`. `bundle.resources` maps ONLY bge + ORT (the reranker is absent and now downloads instead) and points into `crates/vault-embedding/test-fixtures/` — **CI substitutes PLACEHOLDER files there** (`ci.yml:308,614`), so a release built on a CI-style machine ships a stub DLL and dies on the SHA-256 gate at first launch.
> - **(b) First-run model download** — the reranker half is now BUILT (this session). Phi-4's 2.32 GB download already existed. What remains is the UI and the trigger.
> - **(c) `vault-cli` does not ship AT ALL** — no `externalBin`, no sidecar, nothing, so onboarding's `"command": "vault-cli"` snippet is a promise nothing keeps and MCP + nightly consolidation are absent from an installed build. Worse than "not on PATH".
> - **(d) Code signing — DECIDED 2026-07-20: DEFERRED for the closed beta.** No registered company exists (blocks Azure Artifact Signing, which is UK-**organisations**-only; individuals are US/Canada only). And per Microsoft's own docs SmartScreen reputation needs *"several weeks and hundreds of clean installs"* — **30 beta users cannot reach it**, so signing now buys a warning that appears regardless. Tell testers to expect it. Revisit before public launch. **Do NOT buy an EV cert on the "instant SmartScreen reputation" premise** — Microsoft states verbatim that *"EV certificates no longer bypass SmartScreen"* and *"paying a premium for EV solely to avoid SmartScreen warnings is no longer justified."* macOS is covered (founder holds an Apple Developer account); Linux has no trust gate.
> - **(e) ADR-038: the MSI launcher MUST set `LANCE_MEM_POOL_SIZE=268435456`** (WiX pre-args). Dev inherits it from `.cargo/config.toml`; installed builds do NOT. `scripts/run-desktop-dev.ps1` now encodes exactly the env set a launcher needs — **read it as the spec for the installer's launcher**.
> - **(f) Measure RELEASE-build hashing here** (ADR-088 open item) — all timings so far are debug builds. Do not spend a release compile on it before packaging needs one anyway.
>
> ### 🌐 DISTRIBUTION — decided, nothing set up yet
>
> **Website on Hostinger, binaries + models on Cloudflare R2.** R2 charges **$0 egress at any volume**; the whole 3.6 GB payload sits inside the free tier, so the bill is £0 at 30 / 500 / 5,000 users (vs ~$90–180/mo on metered CDNs at 5k). ⚠️ **R2's free `r2.dev` domain is rate-limited and explicitly not for production — a custom domain is REQUIRED.**
> **🔴 BLOCKED ON FOUNDER — needs a DOMAIN NAME.** This gates the R2 setup and the download URL. Also needs: a Cloudflare account (free tier, card on file, bill £0) and the Hostinger plan. Nothing verified about Hostinger's ToS survived research — but with binaries on R2 it only serves a normal marketing site, so it is low-stakes.
> **Serving models straight from Hugging Face `/resolve/` is legitimate and free** (HF names LM Studio / Jan / Ollama as expected consumers, and gives resolvers the highest rate limits) — that is what `model_fetch` does today. Caveat: a consumer installer cannot embed a token, so it sits on the **anonymous per-IP tier**, and a Rust client must handle 429 itself. Moving to R2 removes that dependency; deciding which to ship is open.
>
> **Banked session 21 — UI slice 2:**
> - **Migration `0008_boundaries.sql`** — boundaries become first-class rows instead of a value implied by `memories.boundary`. Backfilled from existing memories (`created_at` = `MIN(created_at)` of that boundary's memories, i.e. when the boundary actually came into being); `default` always seeded so the list is never empty. **No FK** from `memories.boundary` → `boundaries.name`: a failed registry write must never block a memory write (recall is sacrosanct).
> - **`vault-storage/src/boundary_store.rs`** — `list_boundaries` (LEFT JOIN so empty boundaries survive; counts exclude superseded + cold-archived) + `create_boundary` (idempotent: `Ok(false)` = already existed).
> - **Six `VaultAdapter` methods** (`list_recent_memories` / `list_boundaries` / `create_boundary` / `list_agents` / `revoke_agent` / `verify_audit_chain` / `total_memory_count`) on the CONCRETE type, deliberately NOT on the `vault_mcp::Adapter` trait — that trait is the surface every connected agent reaches, and vault-wide boundary enumeration there would be a least-privilege regression (§11.2 SP-2). Same rationale as the existing `append_tauri_command_audit`.
> - **`commands.rs` → `commands/` directory** (`memory.rs` / `boundary.rs` / `agent.rs` / `settings.rs` + `mod.rs`) per BRD §5.11's stated file list; the flat file would have hit ~700 lines.
> - **Frontend** — `renderBoundaries` / `renderAgents` / `renderSettings` / `renderMemList` all read real commands; the `mv_recent` localStorage cache is DELETED and cleared on upgrade.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-003 (session 21) — the desktop UI reads across all boundaries.**
> - **Context:** `search_memories` hardcoded `vec![Boundary::default_name()]`. Once the Boundaries tab can list `work` / `personal`, a UI that cannot search them makes the tab decorative.
> - **Decision:** Tauri-layer reads are OWNER-scoped, spanning every registered boundary. `list_recent_memories` uses an unfiltered `MemoryFilter`; `search_memories` resolves its slice from `list_boundaries()`.
> - **Reasoning:** boundaries are mandatory access control scoping *agents* — BRD §11.4.3 rule 5: *"The LLM/agent never sees memories outside its authorized boundary."* The Tauri layer acts as `ActorKind::User`, the vault owner, who is the party that GRANTS agents their scope. Fencing the owner out of their own boundaries protects nothing.
> - **Does NOT weaken §11.4.3:** agent-facing paths still resolve their authorized slice per-request from the capability token (ADR-SEC-001 D4) and none route through these methods. Rules 3/4 (filtering at the storage layer, unbypassable) are untouched — the `boundaries` table is a name registry, never consulted on a read path.
> - **Fail-secure:** if the registry read fails, `search_memories` falls back to `default` alone (SP-4) rather than widening.
>
> **🐛 LIVE-VERIFICATION FINDING (2026-07-20) — the confirmation dialogs could not say no. FIXED.**
> Founder testing `forget` observed the memory delete and the popup appear afterwards. Root cause: the webview renders `window.confirm()` as an **OK-only message box** — there is no Cancel, so the user CANNOT decline and the destructive action proceeds regardless. Both destructive surfaces were affected: `forget` (permanent delete, unrecoverable) and `revoke` (slice-2 code — I copied slice 1's pattern without questioning it). A gate that cannot say no is not a gate.
> **Fix:** in-app promise-based `confirmAction()` (`dist/app.js` + `.confirm-*` markup/styles) that resolves only on a real choice. Cancel is the dominant button AND holds initial focus, so Enter and Esc both mean "don't"; backdrop click cancels; text set via `textContent` (the revoke prompt interpolates an untrusted agent name). The two remaining `alert()` calls are deliberately left — they are error NOTICES, not gates, so OK-only is the correct shape.
> **Lesson for future UI work:** never use `window.confirm` / `window.prompt` in this webview for anything that gates an action. It is not a browser and does not behave like one.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-004 (session 21) — deletion is immediate and permanent for user-initiated deletes; NO 30-day retention. Amends BRD §11.5.4.**
> - **Context:** BRD §11.5.4 specifies soft delete with 30-day retention for undo, then cryptographic shredding. Founder challenged this while reviewing the delete flow: *"if user wants to delete then let them delete."*
> - **Decision:** **user-initiated deletion (UI / owner action) is an immediate hard delete** — `DELETE FROM memories`, no retention, no trash can. This is what is already implemented; this ADR ratifies it as intentional rather than leaving it as undocumented drift from §11.5.4. **Agent-initiated deletion (MCP `memory_delete`) is explicitly CARVED OUT** and remains open — see below.
> - **Reasoning:**
>   1. **Retention contradicts the product promise.** The vault's pitch is user-owned, zero-knowledge storage. Silently retaining a memory the user deliberately deleted — potentially something sensitive — for a month is the behaviour the product is positioned AGAINST. "Delete" must mean delete.
>   2. **Soft delete introduces a resurfacing failure mode.** It would require every read path to filter deleted rows; a single missed filter surfaces a deleted memory to an AI agent mid-conversation. Recall correctness IS the product ([[project_correctness_is_the_product]]) — voluntarily adding a way for deleted content to come back is a bad trade.
>   3. **The mis-click case is already handled** by the real confirmation dialog above, without retaining anything.
>   4. Local-first single-owner vault: no shared/team context where another party's delete needs review.
> - **CARVE-OUT (open, not decided): agent-initiated deletes.** An agent deleting over MCP has NO human in the loop; a misinterpreted instruction could remove memories the user never agreed to lose and might not notice for weeks. A recovery window has genuine value **there** — and is narrower and safer than a blanket policy, since it never retains anything the owner personally chose to destroy. **Founder agreed 2026-07-20 that this is the right shape.** Decide it as part of the write-side agent-permissions arc, not as a deletion-policy change.
> - **Trade-off accepted:** no undo for owner deletes. Mitigated by the confirmation gate + the fact that the UI states plainly that there is no recovery period.
> - **Alternatives rejected:** (a) BRD §11.5.4 as written — 30-day retention for all deletes: contradicts the privacy promise (reason 1). (b) Retention only for "sensitive" memories: requires classifying sensitivity, which we cannot do reliably and which is itself a privacy surface.
> - **NOTE — sync forward-compat:** cross-device sync (deferred, [[project_sync_deferred_until_paying_users]]) needs deletion **tombstones** for CRDT convergence. A tombstone is `{id, deleted_at}` metadata ONLY — it carries no memory content — so it is compatible with this ADR. Do not let "sync needs tombstones" get mis-read as "sync needs retention".
>
> **📐 ADR-087 (session 22) — first-run model acquisition lives at the Tauri layer; the reranker downloads rather than ships. Implements BRD §5.11.**
> - **Context:** the Qwen3 reranker (1.15 GB) had **no acquisition path whatsoever** — not in `bundle.resources`, gitignored, absent from `MODEL_PROVENANCE.md`, and no download script (`grep -i "qwen\|rerank" scripts/setup-dev-env.*` → no hits). The dev copy was provisioned by hand with no repeatable procedure. Separately, `main.rs` hardcoded `rerank_model_path: None`, so the desktop app ran on the cosine gate.
> - **Decision:** model acquisition is a **Tauri-layer** responsibility, and the reranker joins Phi-4 as a **first-run download**, not an installer payload.
> - **Quoting the spec** — BRD §5.11 `vault-tauri`: *"Handles installer concerns (model download, first-run setup)"*, and under its constraints: *"First-run setup: detect missing model files, show download UI, fetch from Hugging Face mirror"* and *"Stub installer pattern: ~10MB initial install, model download on first launch."* This is implementation of an existing spec, not a new architecture. Founder independently proposed the same stub-installer shape 2026-07-20, and the distribution research reached it a third time.
> - **Division of responsibility:** **integrity** (expected SHA-256) stays in `vault-embedding` beside the consuming code — `model_fetch` references `QWEN3_RERANKER_{MODEL,TOKENIZER}_SHA256`, never restates them, so a hash has exactly one home. **Acquisition** (the URL) lives in `vault-tauri`, because where bytes come from is an installer concern; `vault-embedding` stays a pure inference crate handed paths.
> - **Why not put the downloader in `vault-embedding`:** it would need `vault-llm`'s `ensure_model_at_path` (ADR-043), and `vault-embedding → vault-llm` drags llama.cpp + Vulkan/Metal into the embedding path. BRD §2 dependency flow forbids it. `vault-tauri` sits above both and already pulls each transitively via `vault-app`, so the direct deps add no new weight.
> - **Provenance VERIFIED, not assumed (2026-07-20):** live Hub listing of `shawnw3i/Qwen3-Reranker-0.6B-seq-cls-ONNX` confirms `model.onnx` at repo **root** (not under `onnx/`) at **1,192,779,696 bytes** and `tokenizer.json` at **11,422,654 bytes** — byte-identical in size to the validated fixtures, so the pinned hashes will verify. URLs use the `/resolve/` endpoint (HF's documented client download path, most generously rate-limited). Only these **two** files are needed — confirmed by reading `reranker.rs`: `commit_from_file(model_path)` + `Tokenizer::from_file(tokenizer_path)`; the other five files in the fixture dir are unused.
> - **Bug fixed in passing (not a drive-by — it is in code this depends on):** `model_loader.rs` built its in-flight path as `path.with_extension("gguf.partial")`, which **replaces** the extension. Harmless while Phi-4 was the only caller; for `model.onnx` it produced `model.gguf.partial`, and two models in one directory could collide on a single partial name — the same shared-`.partial` class that broke the weekly CI smoke job. Now appends: `model.gguf` → `model.gguf.partial` (byte-identical to prior behaviour, regression-pinned) and `model.onnx` → `model.onnx.partial`.
> - **Absent files yield `None`, never a startup failure.** `Application::new` documents `None` as graceful degradation. Refusing to start because an optional quality component is missing would be worse than ranking on cosine — recall is sacrosanct.
> - **Deliberately NOT in this step:** the download is not triggered at startup. A silent 1.15 GB transfer would make first launch look frozen — the exact failure the distribution research flagged. `model_fetch::ensure_reranker` is the transport; the first-run progress UI is its own piece of work against the "Quiet" design.
> - **DECIDED (founder, 2026-07-20) — download completes before real use, but OVERLAPS onboarding.** Founder's first call was a plain blocking wait ("better experience is let it fully download"); he then refined it, and the refinement is the design: **run the download underneath the existing onboarding flow.** The user connects their agent, creates boundaries and adds a first memory — work they must do anyway — while the fetch completes behind the welcome animation. Same guarantee as blocking (nothing runs on a degraded engine once they are actually using the vault), but no dead time. Founder: *"the first time user is still going to take time setting up stuff like connecting with agent etc... which can also buy us time while the backend re-ranker or heavy stuff loads."*
>   - **Consequences for the progress UI:** (a) real progress with a total, surfaced in the onboarding chrome rather than as a modal blocker; (b) a retry path — a failed download must not strand a user mid-onboarding; (c) onboarding completion must GATE on download completion, so a fast typist cannot outrun the fetch and reach search on the cosine gate; (d) state the honest size before it starts (~1.15 GB reranker; ~3.6 GB total with Phi-4) — no ambush.
>   - **Measured on the founder's machine 2026-07-20:** cold download of the reranker is **96-131s** at ~9-12 MB/s (two live runs). Onboarding plausibly covers that. A slower connection will not be covered, which is exactly why (b) and (c) are requirements rather than polish.
>   - **Rejected:** background download with the app usable on the cosine gate meanwhile — faster to first use, but ships a first impression that is not the product.
>   - **Founder's fallback if the wait proves unavoidable on slow links:** say so honestly on the opening screen ("takes 1-2 minutes to set up"), and note that Managed/BYOK tiers have no local model to fetch at all — the wait genuinely disappears there rather than being artificially gated. That is a real tier difference, not a manufactured one. Fits [[project_three_mode_deployment]].
>
> **📐 ADR-088 (session 22) — model hashing moves off the async runtime to `spawn_blocking`. Found by measurement, not by reading.**
> - **Context:** the live download test (`reranker_download.rs`) reported a **warm cache check of 67.3s** — the cost of re-verifying the 1.15 GB reranker on an already-populated directory. Since `ensure_reranker` is what first-run wiring will call on every launch, that would have put 67s of pure hashing in front of the window appearing — and ~135s, because `Qwen3RerankerProvider::open` independently re-verifies the same file at load time (ADR-020).
> - **The number made no sense, which is what exposed the bug.** 67s for 1.15 GB is ~17 MB/s. Measured on the same machine, same file, `Get-FileHash` (.NET) does **545 MB/s cold / 717 MB/s page-cached**. 17 MB/s is below even unaccelerated software SHA-256 (~150-250 MB/s), so the hash function was never the bottleneck — the file READ was.
> - **Root cause:** `compute_sha256_of_file` read through `tokio::fs` inside the async fn. Every read round-trips through the blocking pool, so a 1.15 GB file became hundreds of scheduler hops. **It was also a standing violation of BRD §2** — *"All I/O is async (tokio). CPU-bound work (ML inference) is sync, called via `spawn_blocking`"* — hashing gigabytes is CPU-bound work that was stalling the runtime while it ran. This was a correctness bug that presented as a performance bug.
> - **Decision:** `compute_sha256_of_file` now delegates to a sync `compute_sha256_of_file_blocking` via `tokio::task::spawn_blocking`, using `std::fs` + `std::io::Read`. Chunk size (8 MB) and semantics unchanged; only WHERE the work runs changed. `JoinError` maps to `VaultLlmError::Io` so the caller-visible error surface is untouched.
> - **Measured result: 67.3s → 20.3s (3.3x).** Applies to Phi-4's 2.32 GB verification too, at twice the file size.
> - **HONEST GAP — the remaining 10x is probably the build profile, and is UNMEASURED.** 20.3s is still ~10x off .NET's 2.1s, but all our numbers are **debug builds**; `Get-FileHash` is always optimised. Rust debug is typically 5-10x slower on tight numeric loops, so the shipped (release) figure is plausibly low single digits — **but that is an inference, not a measurement.** Do NOT quote a release hashing number until one is taken. **Action: measure at packaging time**, when a release build exists anyway; do not spend a release compile on it before then.
> - **What this decision did NOT require:** a size+mtime "remember what we verified" shortcut was drafted and then dropped. It would have traded away tamper-detection (BRD §11.7.5 signed-binary chain) for speed we did not need. **No security property was weakened to fix this.** If a future measurement shows release hashing is still too slow, that trade returns as its own ADR-SEC entry — not as an optimisation.
>
> **📐 ADR-089 (session 23) — an ABSENT optional model degrades; a PRESENT-but-wrong one still fails. First-run acquisition is triggered from onboarding, and the reranker binding is unconditional.**
> - **Context:** ADR-087 built the downloader but nothing called it, so an installed user never obtained the reranker. Wiring the trigger surfaced a structural problem the session-22 opener had diagnosed backwards.
> - **What the opener got wrong (corrected here, with evidence).** §1 warned that `AppConfig` is built once at startup so a download completing later *"will NOT take effect until relaunch"*, and called it *"the single most likely thing to get silently wrong"*. **It is not.** `LazyQwen3Reranker::new` performs **zero disk I/O** — it stores paths only (`reranker_lazy.rs`, and its own test `construction_touches_no_disk`) — and loads on first use through a `OnceCell` that, per its test `rerank_with_docs_attempts_the_load_and_surfaces_errors`, *"must leave the cell cold for retry"* on failure. So a download that lands mid-session IS picked up by the next query. No re-init path, no relaunch, no restructuring of `Application` construction was needed or built.
> - **The real hazard was the inverse, and it was live.** Both rerank consumers propagated failure with `?` — `reranked_retriever.rs::rerank_pool` and `structured_read_pipeline.rs::apply_reranker`. Binding paths to files that had not downloaded yet would therefore have made search and read **hard-error**, not degrade. Worse, it was already reachable without any download work: `mv_onboarded` sends a returning user straight to the home screen (`app.js`), so an onboarding gate can never cover them.
> - **Decision (founder-approved 2026-07-20, plain-English framing):** distinguish the two failure states.
>   1. **Absent** → new `VaultError::ModelUnavailable { component }`. `LazyQwen3Reranker` checks for the two DOWNLOADED files before attempting a load and reports this. Both consumers catch **only** this variant and fall back to the retriever's own order — byte-identical to the path a vault with no reranker configured already takes.
>   2. **Present but wrong** → unchanged. `ModelIntegrityFailed` propagates and fails the query. The ORT dylib is deliberately excluded from the absent-check: it is installer-bundled, so its absence is a corrupt install, not a pending download.
> - **Consequence — the binding becomes unconditional.** `main.rs` now passes `Some(paths)` even when the files are absent, which is what makes a mid-session download take effect without relaunch. This is ONLY safe because of the degrade above; the two changes are one decision, not two.
> - **Security reasoning (BRD §11 re-read in full, per the IPC-layer rule).** Verify-before-use (§11.12 vault-embedding: *"Model files signed and verified before loading"*) is untouched — an absent component is never loaded, so nothing unverified is used. Boundary enforcement (§11.4.3 rules 3/4) is untouched: reranking is post-retrieval REORDERING, and the degrade returns candidates that already passed the storage-layer boundary filter. SP-4 (fail securely) is satisfied by direction: the degrade widens nothing and reveals nothing — it returns *fewer-well-ordered* results from an already-authorised set. **No security property was weakened**, matching ADR-088's standard.
> - **Trade-off accepted:** a user whose download has not completed gets less sharply ranked results with no error shown. Deliberate — a false error teaches "this product is broken", while slightly weaker ordering is invisible and self-healing. `tracing::warn!` records every degraded query for operators.
> - **Alternatives rejected:** (a) construct `Application` after the download — blocks the window behind a 96-131s transfer, killing the founder's overlap design; (b) degrade on ANY rerank error — swallows integrity failures, turning a tamper signal into an unnoticed quality regression; (c) gate onboarding only, leave the error path — does not cover returning users, who never pass the gate.
> - **Bug found in passing (in code this depends on):** `rerank_pool` capped the pool with `truncate` BEFORE reranking, so the degrade path would have returned only the reranker's batch — silently **less** recall than having no reranker at all. Now split-and-rejoin: success drops the tail exactly as before, degrade returns the whole pool. Regression-pinned.
>
> **📐 ADR-090 (session 24) — the desktop app warms its own ranking model, reports readiness honestly, and searches on submit rather than per keystroke. Found by live-verifying ADR-089.**
> - **Context:** session 23's work was gate-green but never run. Running it confirmed ADR-089's central claim and surfaced three things unit tests could not.
> - **Root cause of the slow first search — a STRUCTURAL divergence, not a missing feature.** `Application` has two lifecycle entry points. `start_with_mcp` is documented as the *"**Production lifecycle entry point**"* and warms the reranker at step 3b (`application.rs`, `reranker.spawn_warmup()`). `start` is documented verbatim as the *"**Test-focused entry point**"* and spawns only the retry worker. **The desktop app calls `start`** — it must, because `start_with_mcp` blocks on an rmcp `initialize` handshake that never arrives in a GUI (ADR-034). So the GUI runs its production lifecycle on the path labelled test-focused, and silently misses anything ever added to the other one.
>   - **This is the THIRD instance of that exact shape**, and naming it is the point of this ADR: (1) session 22 — `rerank_model_path: None` hardcoded, so the desktop ranked on the cosine gate; (2) session 23 — the downloader built but never called; (3) here — `spawn_warmup` built but never reached. Each was invisible to tests and each was found by RUNNING the app. **Before adding anything to `start_with_mcp`, check whether the desktop path needs it too.**
> - **Decision 1 — warm-up is explicit at the call site, not folded into `start()`.** New `Application::spawn_reranker_warmup() -> bool`, called from `main.rs` inside the existing `tauri::async_runtime::block_on` (it uses `tokio::spawn`, which panics outside a runtime — the same reason `start()` is called there). Folding it into `start()` would have made every test that configures a reranker load a 1.2 GB model as a side effect of starting, and would have blurred the Path α discipline that keeps the two entry points named rather than flag-switched.
> - **Decision 2 — open the window instantly; report readiness honestly (founder, 2026-07-22).** Founder's framing: *"we can set animation where user only lands on screen once the model is ready … so nothing is dishonest we are telling the truth"*. Presented with the measured cost of gating — **~16 s integrity verify + ~24 s load on EVERY launch, ~2.5 min on first run** — he chose the non-blocking variant: window opens immediately, a quiet line says the engine is still getting ready, recall works throughout on the retriever's own order. **This preserves ADR-089's explicit rejection of alternative (a)** ("construct `Application` after the download — blocks the window behind a 96-131s transfer"); gating would have reintroduced exactly that.
>   - New `RerankerState { NotConfigured, Unavailable, Preparing, Ready }` + wire strings. **`Preparing` is the only state that is a wait**; showing a spinner for `Unavailable` (download not landed) or `NotConfigured` (no engine) would promise a wait that never ends — a permanent lie, worse than the silence it replaced.
>   - Commands `recall_engine_state` (side-effect free — a readiness query must never trigger the load it is reporting on) and `warm_recall_engine` (idempotent). The frontend calls the latter after acquisition resolves: on a genuine first run the files were still downloading when `main.rs` made its startup attempt, so that attempt correctly fails fast and leaves the cell cold.
> - **Decision 3 — a vault search runs on ENTER, not per keystroke.** The old 350 ms debounce stopped keystroke *spam* but still fired a real query on every natural typing pause, and each became a full ranking pass. **Measured live 2026-07-22:** typing `work` ran **4** passes, `keyboard` ran **3**; a single warm pass is **~4.3-4.9 s**, so the founder's "26 second" search was three passes stacked, not one slow one. `renderMemList`'s existing sequence guard discarded the stale *responses* — the work was already paid for.
>   - **Founder raised the hosted-tier cost question and it is the right one.** No LLM tokens are involved in any tier (the LLM is out of the read path by lock), so this is **compute, not tokens** — and it only costs *us* in Managed, where the ranking runs on our hardware. **Agents over MCP were never affected**: they send one query per question rather than typing, so the primary traffic surface was always 1:1.
>   - Clearing the box still updates instantly — returning to the recent list is a cheap unfiltered read, not a search.
> - **Trade-off accepted:** no live type-ahead against the vault. Deliberate — reranking is a semantic judgement, not a prefix filter, and running it on partial words was producing rankings for strings the user never meant to ask about.
> - **Security:** no new surface. Both commands take **no parameters** (§11.7.1 — the strongest validation is input that does not exist), touch no vault state and read no memory, so neither writes an audit row (§11.9.1, same reasoning as `ensure_recall_engine`). Readiness strings name no model, runtime or vendor (ADR-086), pinned by tests on both sides. No crypto path touched; verify-before-use unchanged (an absent component is still never loaded).
> - **Alternatives rejected:** (a) gate the window on readiness — founder's first framing; rejected on the measured ~40 s/launch cost once the number was known, and it would have re-introduced the ADR-089 alternative already rejected. (b) Fold warm-up into `start()` — silently loads 1.2 GB in tests. (c) Keep per-keystroke search with a longer debounce — still N passes for one question; the problem is *firing on partial input*, not the interval. (d) Suppress the progress row on a warm run (my first proposal) — the founder's counter-proposal of **telling the truth instead of hiding it** is better, and is what shipped.
> - **Still unverified at ADR time:** network-kill mid-fetch → degraded completion. Deliberately not tested live: the expensive half of that risk (absent model breaking search) is now PROVEN fixed, and testing the remainder costs deleting 1.15 GB, re-downloading, and cutting the network for a cosmetic error row. Carried as the one open item from the session-23 verification list.
>
> **📐 First-run trigger (ADR-089, same commit).**
> - **`commands/engine.rs`** — `ensure_recall_engine`, a Tauri command taking **no parameters**. Destination, URL and hashes are all fixed at build time, so there is no caller-supplied path and thus no arbitrary-file-write surface (§11.7.1: the strongest validation is input that does not exist). Failure returns the opaque code `recall_engine_unavailable`; details stay in the log (§11.7.2 — an error built from the URL would have leaked the model repo into the UI, breaking ADR-086 too).
> - **Dedupe + retry come from one `tokio::sync::OnceCell`**: concurrent callers share a single transfer (the frontend starts it on the welcome screen and awaits it again at the gate), and a failed attempt leaves the cell cold, so retry falls out of the type rather than needing a flag.
> - **Progress seam** — `ensure_model_at_path_with_progress` in `vault-llm` (the plain form is now a thin wrapper, so **Phi-4's caller is unchanged**), aggregated across both files by `ensure_reranker_with_progress`. Byte-quantised at 8 MB (~143 events for 1.15 GB): deterministic cadence, no clock read per chunk, no event flood. A cached file jumps the counter rather than reporting a transfer it never made.
> - **UI** — a FOURTH boot-check row that appears only while bytes are actually moving, reusing the existing check-row machinery rather than inventing a progress widget. Honest-UI: no row at all when the files were already present.
> - **The gate** — `finishOnboarding` awaits acquisition; both exits from onboarding route through it. On failure it lets the user through DEGRADED rather than stranding them mid-onboarding (ADR-087 requirement (b)), which is precisely what the degrade decision above makes safe.
> - **New contract guard** — the progress event name is an unchecked string on both sides of the language boundary; a rename would leave the download working and the bar silently frozen. `frontend_contract.rs` now cross-checks it, plus a white-label assertion on it.
>
> **📐 ADR-091 (session 25) — MSI packaging: ship `vault-cli` via a WiX File component, set the env var + PATH via WiX, build locally with vcvars. Implements BRD §5.11 / T0.2.14.**
> - **Context:** the app was fully built and live-proven but had NO installer — `tauri.conf.json` still said "V0.1 alpha / LanceDB plaintext", `vault-cli` didn't ship at all, and the launcher env (ADR-038) was undefined for installed builds. First MSI ever bundled this session.
> - **Config fixes:** version 0.1.0→0.2.0; white-label privacy-first descriptions (the old copy would have told a beta user their vault is plaintext — false and trust-destroying). The `resources` map was already correct (bge trio ships; reranker downloads).
> - **`vault-cli` ships via a WiX File component (`windows/fragments/installer.wxs`), NOT `bundle.externalBin`.** externalBin is compile-time-validated (would need per-triple CI placeholder binaries, tauri #14602) AND derives a WiX Component `@Id` from the binary name — the hyphen in "vault-cli" is an illegal WiX identifier and hits candle **CNDL0014** (tauri #14681, closed "rename the binary"). A hand-authored File component lets us set a legal `@Id` ourselves and ship the natural `vault-cli.exe` filename. Bonus: the fragment is bundle-time-only, so it has **ZERO effect on CI** (which never runs `tauri build`). The install dir id is `INSTALLDIR` (confirmed against the generated `main.wxs`); a file-installing component sits under `<DirectoryRef Id="INSTALLDIR">` and must NOT also carry `Component/@Directory` (that pair is CNDL0062).
> - **`LANCE_MEM_POOL_SIZE` (ADR-038) + PATH via WiX `<Environment>`.** The env var is per-user, covering BOTH the app and the MCP `vault-cli` path (a GUI-launcher wrapper would miss the CLI, which opens the same lance store). The install dir is appended to user PATH so the UNCHANGED cross-agent `vault-cli mcp serve` snippet resolves — chosen over rewriting the snippet to an absolute backslash path, which is less portable across MCP clients. The var CANNOT be set in Rust (ADR-002 forbids unsafe; `std::env::set_var` is unsafe) — WiX is the correct home per ADR-038.
> - **The shippable MSI MUST be built locally.** CI `touch`es 0-byte placeholder model fixtures (for tauri-build's compile-time resource validation), so a CI-built MSI would ship dead files and die at the integrity gate. Documented constraint, not a defect.
> - **Two build-environment lessons (each cost a failed build, both banked to [[feedback_cargo_on_windows_use_powershell]]):** (1) a fresh/cleaned release build needs VS2019 BuildTools vcvars activated or `openssl-src` (vendored OpenSSL, ADR-006) fails ~crate 527 — cc-rs auto-detects `cl` but openssl's own `nmake` build needs full vcvars on PATH; (2) the two heavy C++ CMake crates (llama.cpp + DuckDB) OOM a 16 GB machine when built concurrently — fixed by freeing WSL/Docker (~3.2 GB, Docker Desktop was idle) + `-j 1` (never two big crates at once) + `CMAKE_BUILD_PARALLEL_LEVEL=2` (CMake spawns internal jobs that ignore cargo `-j`).
> - **VERIFIED end-to-end on the installed build (2026-07-23):** MSI admin-extract + registry read (env var + PATH written, all files real/non-stub); installed app loads the bundled bge model from `C:\Program Files\Memory Vault\` (integrity OK); and a full fresh-first-run — moved dev vault + WebView state aside → empty vault + onboarding → reranker absent degrades cleanly (ADR-089) → first-run download of the 1.19 GB reranker from HF + SHA-256 verified → recall engine ready + warm with no relaunch (ADR-089/090).
> - **Open (non-blocking):** the File `@Source` is an absolute local path (portable/CI bundle must templatize); code signing deferred (SmartScreen warning expected for testers); blocker (f) release-hashing timing now measurable from a warm installed launch. The founder's dev vault is safe aside as `%APPDATA%/%LOCALAPPDATA%\com.shahbaz242630.memory-vault.dev-backup` (restore or delete at will — throwaway dogfood data).
>
> **📐 ADR-092 (session 26) — a dedicated cross-platform `vault-scheduler` crate: one `Scheduler` trait, three per-OS backends, per-user (no elevation). Implements the founder's 2026-07-23 pre-beta directive + BRD §2 / §5.6-scheduling.**
> - **Context:** the founder asked for a user-configurable automatic-maintenance schedule (its own tab + an onboarding page, Windows/macOS/Linux). Linux has no single scheduler; Windows has no cron. Modelled as a leaf capability per BRD §2 (traits, no cycles).
> - **Decision:** new leaf crate `vault-scheduler` depending only on `vault-core`. A **synchronous** `Scheduler` trait (`register`/`unregister`/`status`) with a compile-time-selected backend per target: **Windows** Task Scheduler via a **task-XML** definition through `schtasks /Create /XML` (NOT a `/TR` string — separates program from args, and lets us set `RunLevel=LeastPrivilege`+`InteractiveToken` = per-user no-elevation, and `StartWhenAvailable=true` = OS-level missed-run catch-up); **macOS** launchd (`~/Library/LaunchAgents` plist + `launchctl bootstrap gui/<uid>`; `StartCalendarInterval` wake-catch-up; native `EnvironmentVariables`); **Linux** systemd `--user` timer+service (`OnCalendar` + `Persistent=true` catch-up) **with a cron fallback** (single-quoted, no catch-up) when no systemd user session is reachable.
> - **Why synchronous + `spawn_blocking`:** registering a task is a short bounded subprocess call, not long I/O; the trait stays sync (no tokio dep in the leaf crate) and the async Tauri layer calls it via `spawn_blocking` — matches BRD §2's "blocking work is sync, dispatched via spawn_blocking".
> - **Cross-platform test coverage without dead code:** each backend module is `#[cfg(any(<os>, test))]` — a real build compiles only the native backend (no dead code, no `allow` hacks), but `cargo test` on ANY platform compiles + exercises all three PURE builders (the plist/XML/unit-file/crontab construction — where correctness + injection-safety live). Only the shell-out `imp` structs are gated to their exact OS.
> - **Windows LIVE-PROVEN (2026-07-24, founder's box):** an ignored round-trip integration test registered a real per-user task; Task Scheduler's own `/Query` reported it (`Next Run Time 3:00 AM`, `Logon Mode: Interactive only`, `Run As User: shahb`, program+args clean, 2-hour `ExecutionTimeLimit`); unregister removed it. mac/Linux `imp`s compile clean but their live `launchctl`/`systemctl`/`crontab` behaviour is CI/beta-pending — **NOT claimed proven**.
> - **New `VaultError::Scheduler(String)`** category in vault-core (scheduling is a first-class subsystem, not a Config/Io sub-case). Additive; the two DELIBERATELY-exhaustive matches in `vault-mcp` (`audit.rs::from_vault_error`, `server.rs::vault_error_to_mcp`) got a Scheduler arm (→ generic internal, since scheduling never reaches MCP dispatch). Spec-validation failures map to `InvalidInput`, not `Scheduler` — caught before any OS call. (Lesson banked: my first cascade check missed these two — they were in my own grep output; open the files, don't just list them.)
>
> **📐 ADR-093 (session 26) — the desktop app never loads Phi-4; both scheduled and manual consolidation spawn the bundled `vault-cli consolidate run` as a subprocess. Phi-4 downloads for everyone during onboarding, non-gating.**
> - **Context:** consolidation runs on Phi-4 (2.49 GB), and the desktop `AppConfig` had `phi4_model_path: None` (consolidator unwired). Wiring Phi-4 in-process would eagerly load 2.49 GB at `Application::new` on every launch — the exact startup cost ADR-090 fought.
> - **Decision:** the desktop app does NOT host Phi-4. Both the scheduled 3 AM run and the "Run now" button invoke the **bundled `vault-cli consolidate run`** (ADR-091 ships it) in its own short-lived process, pointed at the app's exact vault paths (`--vault-db <data>/vault.db --vector-dir <data>/lance --graph-db <data>/graph.duckdb consolidate --bge-model … --phi4-model … run` — **flags not env**, because Windows Task Scheduler cannot set per-task env). One consolidation code path for scheduled + manual; the app process stays light. This IS the documented V0.2 deployment.
> - **Single-writer safety (free):** `vault-cli consolidate` takes the vault-owner lock (ADR-SEC-002). If an MCP agent is connected (holds the lock), the run fails fast → surfaced as `maintenance_vault_busy` → UI says "will run at the next opportunity", never corrupts. The desktop UI itself is not a vault-owner, so "Run now" while the app is open is safe.
> - **Phi-4 acquisition (founder 2026-07-24 — "always during onboarding"):** `model_fetch::ensure_phi4[_with_progress]` mirrors the reranker downloader, referencing `vault_llm::Phi4MiniConfig`'s pinned constants (no restated hash). It downloads for EVERY user during onboarding but is **NON-gating** — the recall engine (reranker) still gates `finishOnboarding` because recall needs it; Phi-4 streams in the background because only maintenance needs it. `MaintenanceEngineFetch` (a second `OnceCell`) dedupes it; `maintenance-engine://progress` reports it.
> - **Trade-off / tech-debt:** every install downloads ~2.49 GB it may not use if the user never enables maintenance. Accepted per founder ("give every beta tester the real product ready") — background + non-gating. It currently fires on every launch (short-circuits if present), so a returning user who lacks it gets a background fetch; revisit if beta finds it heavy.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-005 (session 26) — scheduler injection-safety gate, no secrets on the command line, per-run audit, uninstall removal. BRD §11 re-read for the new subprocess + OS-registration surface.**
> - **The surface:** we register an OS task whose command line carries file paths, and (cron) execute through `/bin/sh`. The threat is a path/arg breaking out of its field to inject a directive or a second command.
> - **Injection-safety gate (tested FIRST, before any backend):** `TaskId::new` restricts the task id to `A-Za-z0-9._-` (excludes every separator / metacharacter / quote / whitespace); `ScheduleSpec::validate` rejects any CONTROL character in the label / args / env-values (newline = the crontab/plist/unit line-break injection vector) and constrains env-var NAMES to `A-Za-z0-9_`. Spaces + ordinary punctuation in PATHS are allowed and handled by per-backend escaping. Adversarial tests per backend: schtasks-XML entity-escape + Windows `CommandLineToArgvW` arg-quoting, launchd XML-escape, systemd `%`/`$` doubling + quote, POSIX single-quote (a `$(rm -rf ~)` arg survives only inside single quotes).
> - **No secrets on the command line:** the scheduled `vault-cli` reads the master key from the OS keychain at run time (verified in `build_application`); the spec carries only model paths + `LANCE_MEM_POOL_SIZE`. No key/passphrase is ever passed as an arg or env by us.
> - **Audit (§11.9.1):** `set_maintenance_schedule` (settings change) and `run_maintenance_now` (consolidation run) each write a `TauriCommandInvoke` audit row via the adapter (`ActorKind::User`). `get_maintenance_schedule` / `ensure_maintenance_engine` write none (read-only / no vault state — same posture as the engine commands).
> - **White-label (ADR-086):** every user-visible string, error code (`maintenance_*`), and event (`maintenance-engine://progress`) is stack-name-free; a `frontend_contract` test pins the event both ways.
> - **No security property weakened:** scheduling reads no memory and enforces no boundary itself; the consolidation it triggers runs the existing, already-audited consolidator. Verify-before-use unchanged. Matches ADR-088/089's standard.
> - **Uninstall (~~OPEN~~ → ✅ CLOSED session 30 by ADR-SEC-006, below):** the registered task is now removed on uninstall by a WiX custom action in the ADR-091 fragment. Code, guards and MSI-table verification are done; the live install/uninstall run is the session-31 opener.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-008 (session 31) — uninstall KEEPS your memories, the app says so plainly, and "Delete everything" cryptographically erases them. Same arc as ADR-SEC-007; the two ship together.**
> - **The question that started it (founder, 2026-08-14):** *"when the vault is uninstalled by the user — does data not get removed? or does it also get deleted?"* Answer, measured rather than assumed: **the installer never touches vault data.** `installer.wxs` has no `RemoveFile`/`RemoveFolder` for the data directory, and the folder read 3525.2 MB both before and after the ADR-SEC-006 uninstall — byte-identical. The Credential Manager entry survives too, so a reinstall restores a fully working vault (which is exactly what happened).
> - **INDUSTRY EVIDENCE (researched, not assumed).** Windows Installer's own rule is that an uninstall must *"leave no useless parts of itself behind"* — and it **explicitly carves out user data**, which persists unless the author writes a custom action to delete it. MSIX deletes everything; MSI does not. The cautionary tale is **Signal**, whose Windows uninstaller DID delete user data: a user lost their entire message history and argued *"user data should NEVER be deleted without the user's explicit permission"*; the issue was closed **not planned**. The closest analogue to us is **1Password** (encrypted local vault, key in Credential Manager) — it leaves vault caches behind and tells users to clear the Credential Manager entry by hand. **Obsidian** leaves data but offers an in-app "discard local database". Conclusion: **keeping data on uninstall is correct and near-universal; keeping it SILENTLY is the part we were getting wrong.**
> - **Decision (three parts):** (a) uninstall continues to leave data — deleting a year of memories as a side effect of a routine uninstall is unrecoverable loss, and people uninstall to troubleshoot; (b) the Settings tab now **states plainly** that uninstalling removes the app and not the memories, and shows the exact folder so the claim is checkable; (c) a deliberate **"Delete everything"** action performs cryptographic erasure. **Explicitly REJECTED: a "delete my data?" checkbox in the Windows uninstaller** — uninstall flows get click-through'd and the mistake is unrecoverable.
> - **The mechanism was already specified — BRD §11.5.4:** *"Account deletion: master key is destroyed, making all encrypted data permanently unrecoverable."* Since every at-rest key (`at_rest_key`, `sqlcipher_passphrase`) is a BLAKE3 subkey of the single 32-byte master_key in Credential Manager, destroying that key kills SQLCipher, the Lance store, the sealed graph AND the newly-sealed REPORTs at once. NIST SP 800-88 classes **cryptographic erase as Purge-level**, and unlike file deletion it is not defeated by journaling, SSD wear-levelling, block remapping, or a backup of the data folder taken last week.
> - **⚠️ THE ORDERING IS THE WHOLE DESIGN: destroy the KEY first, the FILES second.** The two orders fail completely differently under interruption, so this is a contract in `erasure.rs` rather than an implementation detail:
>
>   | order | interrupted halfway | result |
>   |---|---|---|
>   | key → files | files remain | data is cryptographically dead ✅ |
>   | files → key | key remains | any surviving copy still decrypts ❌ |
>
>   If the key step fails, **no files are touched at all** and the command returns an error: erasure either succeeds at the step that matters or does nothing.
> - **Erasure never opens the vault it is destroying.** `erase_vault` takes plain paths + identifiers, never an `Application` or a live `StorageBackend` — a wipe that requires a healthy vault is useless exactly when it is needed (corrupt database, half-migrated state, key that no longer matches the data). Founder-adjacent call taken inline and stated.
> - **`VAULT_ENTRIES` is an explicit allow-list, not a recursive delete of the vault directory.** The directory is caller-supplied, and a bug in that path must never become an unbounded `remove_dir_all` of, say, `%APPDATA%`. A test pins every entry to a single relative path component with no `..`. **`models/` is deliberately NOT erased** — ~3.5 GB of ML model files containing no user data; erasing memories must not force a multi-GB re-download.
> - **The delete step is VERIFIED, not assumed.** `destroy_master_key` re-probes the credential after deleting and returns an error if the secret is still readable. A `delete_credential` that silently did nothing would otherwise have us telling the user their vault was erased while every byte remained decryptable.
> - **NO audit row, and that is deliberate.** BRD §11.9.1 requires state-changing operations to be audited, but this operation destroys the database the audit log lives in — writing a row and then shredding its table records nothing. It is logged via `tracing` to the application log, which survives. Noted rather than silently skipped.
> - **UI: type-to-confirm, not a yes/no dialog.** The user types `DELETE`; the button is disabled until it matches exactly. A yes/no box is too easy to click through for an action that cannot be undone even from a backup. `frontend_contract.rs` pins the gate — and pins that the phrase check appears **before** the `invoke`, since a guard that runs after the call is decorative. (Session 21's lesson still applies underneath: this webview renders native `confirm` as OK-only, so native dialogs cannot gate anything.)
> - **The failure message tells the truth.** If erasure fails the UI says the memories were **NOT** deleted and are **still readable** — softer wording would be a lie about the single property the user was trying to obtain. Undeletable leftover files are reported as a *disk-space* fact and explicitly do **not** downgrade the confidentiality result, because once the key is gone those bytes are unreadable ciphertext.
> - **ADR-086 carve-out applies here.** Naming AES-256 and the OS credential store is permitted for security specifics, and on this surface it is *owed*: a user destroying their data is entitled to know what "deleted" actually means.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-007 (session 31) — the consolidator REPORT is SEALED at rest. It was writing verbatim memory text to disk in the clear, in violation of BRD §11.5.1's "no exceptions".**
> - **How it was found — it was NOT a code review.** The founder asked a product question: *"when the vault is uninstalled, does the data get removed?"* Answering it honestly meant checking what is actually on disk after an uninstall, and the sweep of `%APPDATA%\com.shahbaz242630.memory-vault` returned: `vault.db` ✅ encrypted (SQLCipher — header is random bytes, not `SQLite format 3`), `graph.sealed` ✅ encrypted, the whole `lance/` vector store ✅ encrypted, `.vault.lock` + `maintenance.json` plaintext but content-free — and **`reports/personal.report.json` PLAINTEXT, containing verbatim fact text, memory ids, `as_of` timestamps and confidences.** Read with a plain `Get-Content`, no key involved. **Generalisable: a question about product behaviour walked straight onto a security bug that no amount of reading the security section would have found, because §11 says what MUST be true and the disk says what IS true.**
> - **The violated rule is unambiguous.** BRD §11.5.1: *"All data on disk is encrypted. No exceptions."* Not a judgement call, not a trade-off — a spec violation. The threat model covers it explicitly (§11.1, "Compromised endpoints — Access our app's data files on disk").
> - **ROOT CAUSE — a checklist-coverage gap, not carelessness.** BRD §11.12's *"No plaintext data on disk, ever"* is listed under **vault-storage**. The REPORT is written by **vault-consolidator** and read by **vault-retrieval**, neither of which carries that line. The artifact was born in crates whose security checklist never mentioned at-rest encryption, so every individual crate was compliant with its own checklist while the vault as a whole was not. **Per-crate checklists cannot catch an artifact that crosses crates.** (Tech-debt (f) below proposes the workspace-wide guard.)
> - **Decision: reuse the EXISTING envelope, add no crypto (SP-5).** `vault_storage::{seal_vault_blob, unseal_vault_blob}` are thin public wrappers over the already-shipped `seal_file_bytes`/`unseal_file_bytes` — the same XChaCha20-Poly1305 dryoc envelope, the same `version || granularity || header || ciphertext` framing, the same `"vault-at-rest-v1"` domain separator that seals the vector store and the graph. There is no second crypto path to review.
> - **The boundary binding came free, which is the neat part.** AAD is already `BLAKE3("vault-at-rest-v1" || relative_path)`, and the REPORT's relative path is `reports/<boundary>.report.sealed` — so **the boundary name is inside the AAD by construction**. That satisfies BRD §11.3.2 ("AAD includes … boundary … prevents swap attacks") with no new mechanism: copying `work.report.sealed` over `personal.report.sealed` changes the AAD, AEAD authentication fails, and the read fails closed instead of serving one boundary's facts under another's name — which would have defeated the mandatory access control in §11.4.3.
> - **Layout moved DOWN to `vault_storage::report_layout`, and that is a correctness decision rather than tidiness.** The filename and the AAD string are needed by the writer (`vault-consolidator`) and the reader (`vault-retrieval`), which do not depend on each other; `report_io.rs` had therefore hand-duplicated `REPORTS_DIRNAME` with a comment explaining why. That was fine while the filename was the only shared fact. **Once the path became AAD input, a one-character divergence between the two copies would seal fine, write fine, and then fail EVERY unseal — degrading silently to `REPORT_MISSING` on every read, with no error at the point of the mistake and a green build.** One definition, in the crate both sides already depend on, removes the failure mode by construction. The original "no sibling dependency" rationale is preserved: `vault-storage` is a shared lower layer, not a sibling.
> - **The extension changed `.report.json` → `.report.sealed`, deliberately.** A `.json` file that is really an encrypted envelope invites a future reader to `serde_json` it, fail, and "fix" the failure with a plaintext fallback — reintroducing this exact bug. Distinct extensions make the two formats impossible to confuse, and a test asserts the path does not end in `.json`.
> - **NO plaintext fallback on the read path, ever.** Unseal failure (wrong key / tampering / wrong boundary) logs at ERROR and returns `Ok(None)`, degrading through the existing `REPORT_MISSING` path — because an unauthenticatable REPORT is exactly as trustworthy as no REPORT (SP-4). A legacy `.report.json` left on disk is **not** read as a fallback, pinned by `legacy_plaintext_report_is_never_read`.
> - **MIGRATION IS THE HALF THAT ACTUALLY CLOSES THE HOLE.** Shipping the sealed writer alone fixes only future runs: the new writer uses a different filename, so every existing `reports/<boundary>.report.json` would sit there in the clear forever. `migrate_plaintext_reports` runs on **every** startup (idempotent; no version marker, because a marker that got out of step would silently skip the sweep and the sweep is cheap), re-seals the content where it can, and **deletes the plaintext unconditionally — including on the failure path**. An unreadable REPORT is worthless, and preserving a worthless file in the clear is strictly worse than losing it; the next consolidation regenerates it. It also destroys leftover `.report.json.tmp` files, which carry the same fact text and which a crash-mid-write leaves behind.
> - **Founder decisions taken inline (both were mine to call, stated not blocked on):** (1) migrate by SEALING rather than plain deletion, so recall quality is not degraded until the next consolidation; (2) the forthcoming delete-everything action must work even when the vault cannot be opened — a wipe that needs a healthy vault is useless exactly when it is needed.
> - **Tests are security-first per BRD §11.13** — adversarial before functional: wrong key, tampered ciphertext, truncated envelope, cross-boundary swap, legacy-plaintext-never-read, migration-destroys-plaintext-even-when-unresealable. **The load-bearing one is `sealed_bytes_do_not_contain_the_plaintext` / `written_report_is_not_readable_on_disk`:** a sealing wrapper that silently did nothing would pass every round-trip test ever written, so the suite asserts the property the feature exists for — the secret is not in the bytes — and each such test carries a control proving it is testing encryption rather than an empty file. Same discipline as ADR-SEC-006's comment-stripping guard, which passed on prose until it was made to run against real markup.
> - **Scope — stated, not silently skipped:** this ADR covers the REPORT only. `maintenance.json` (schedule config) and `.vault.lock` remain plaintext and are content-free by inspection; they are noted, not fixed. Part B (crypto-shred delete-everything + honest uninstall messaging) is the same arc and lands with this work.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-006 (session 30) — uninstall removes the registered maintenance task via a WiX custom action driving `schtasks`, not our own binary. Closes ADR-SEC-005's last open item.**
> - **The gap was live, not theoretical.** On the founder's machine `\com.memoryvault.maintenance` was **still registered** from a session-27 test: `Status: Ready` · `Next Run Time 29/07/2026 5:25 AM` · `Last Run 28/07/2026 10:05:51` · **`Last Result: 1`** · `Task To Run: C:\Projects\GitHub\Memory Vault\target\debug\vault-cli.exe …`. A task that outlived whatever created it by weeks, firing daily into a repo path we have deleted and rebuilt several times, failing every morning, and nobody knew. A beta tester who installs, tries the feature and uninstalls gets exactly this. For a product whose promise is "you own your data and nothing is left behind", leaving an OS-level job behind is squarely the wrong behaviour.
> - **Decision:** a `<CustomAction>` in `windows/fragments/installer.wxs` runs `"[SystemFolder]schtasks.exe" /Delete /TN "com.memoryvault.maintenance" /F` during uninstall.
> - **WHY `schtasks` AND NOT our own `vault-cli`** (the tempting choice, since `vault_scheduler::Scheduler::unregister` is already written and tested): **during an uninstall our binary is the thing being deleted.** Driving removal through it means sequencing before `RemoveFiles`, and it could be file-locked, already gone, or crash — and a crash there fails the USER'S UNINSTALL. `schtasks.exe` ships with Windows, is always present, and is unaffected by our own removal. vault-scheduler still owns removal at RUN time; this is purely the teardown path, where that crate is unavailable by construction.
> - **WHY `NOT UPGRADINGPRODUCTCODE` — the non-obvious one, and the reason this needed thought rather than a one-liner.** `REMOVE="ALL"` is **also true while a major upgrade removes the OLD product**. Conditioned on `REMOVE` alone, **every app update would silently switch the user's automatic maintenance off and never say so** — a data-adjacent behaviour change with no notification. `UPGRADINGPRODUCTCODE` is set only during that removal-during-upgrade pass, so excluding it preserves the schedule across upgrades and clears it only on a real uninstall.
> - **`Return="ignore"`:** `schtasks /Delete` exits non-zero when the task is absent (never scheduled, or already removed). That must never fail an uninstall — matching the idempotent posture `unregister` already has in Rust.
> - **`Execute="immediate"`, not deferred:** the task is per-user, created by the interactive user with `LogonType=InteractiveToken` (verified session 27), so removal belongs in that user's context; immediate execution also resolves `[SystemFolder]` normally, which a deferred action cannot. **Accepted limit:** if user A installs and user B uninstalls, A's task survives. Deferred-as-SYSTEM would cover it but cannot resolve properties. Single-owner desktop vault — not worth the complexity today, logged as tech debt (b4).
